Since $F_x>0$ and its endpoint limits are infinite with the indicated signs, <a href="#fiber:inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:inverse">[fiber:inverse]</a> determines a unique $\tau>0$ for every $y\ne\zeta$.

<a id="fiber:curvature-convention"></a>
Hereafter $C=-R$ denotes the negative Hermitian curvature tensor: $$\label{fiber:curvature-convention}
 C_{\alpha\bar\beta\gamma\bar\delta}
 =\partial_\alpha\partial_{\bar\beta}g_{\gamma\bar\delta}
 -g^{\mu\bar\nu}
   (\partial_\alpha g_{\gamma\bar\nu})
   (\partial_{\bar\beta}g_{\mu\bar\delta}).$$ The sectional-curvature sign associated with this convention is verified below; in particular, positivity of the relevant contractions of $C$ means nonpositive Riemannian sectional curvature.

<a id="stmt-fiber-metric"></a>
<div id="fiber:metric" class="lemma">

<a id="fiber:metric-form"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Metric and regularity of the Legendre transform). *Write $$P=\tau q,\qquad V=(B_{i\bar j}),\qquad
 G=(\psi_{i\bar j}),\qquad h=(U_{i\bar j}),\qquad
 \mathcal N=h_\tau.$$ Where $h>0$, the complex Hessian of $\ell_{\mathrm{rad}}$ is $$\label{fiber:metric-form}
 h_{i\bar j}\,ds_i\,d\bar s_j+
 P\left|d\log(y-\zeta)+U_{\tau i}\,ds_i\right|^2.$$ It is positive off the axis. Its spatial Schur complement is exactly $h$. The potential and its complex Hessian extend smoothly across $y=\zeta$; on that axis the Hessian in the original splitting is $V\oplus e^\psi$, and is positive whenever $V>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fiber-metric"><summary>Proof of stmt-fiber-metric</summary>

*Proof.* Use the holomorphic coordinate $z_0=\log(y-\zeta)$, so that $\rho=z_0+\bar z_0$. Differentiating $U_\tau+\rho=0$ gives $$U_{\tau\tau}=-P^{-1},\qquad
 \partial_{z_0}\tau=P,\qquad
 \partial_{s_i}\tau=P U_{\tau i}.$$ Differentiation of $\ell=U+\tau\rho$ now gives $$g_{0\bar0}=P,\qquad g_{i\bar0}=P U_{\tau i},\qquad
 g_{i\bar j}=h_{i\bar j}+P U_{\tau i}U_{\tau\bar j},$$ which is <a href="#fiber:metric-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:metric-form">[fiber:metric-form]</a>. For regularity, write $F=\log\tau+f(\tau,s)$. The equation $f_\tau=(q^{-1}-1)/\tau$, with $f(0,s)=0$, shows that $f$ is smooth. Equation <a href="#fiber:inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:inverse">[fiber:inverse]</a> becomes $\tau e^{f(\tau,s)}=e^\psi|y-\zeta|^2$. Its derivative with respect to $\tau$ at zero is one, so the smooth implicit-function theorem applies. In particular, $\tau=e^\psi|y-\zeta|^2+O(|y-\zeta|^4)$. Substitution in <a href="#fiber:potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:potential">[fiber:potential]</a> proves the stated extension and axis metric. ◻

</details>

We use a local holomorphic change $z_0\mapsto z_0+f(s)$ to make $U_{\tau i}=0$ at a specified point. The corresponding change of $U$ is linear in $\tau$ with a pluriharmonic spatial coefficient; it leaves $h$ and $\mathcal N$ unchanged. The quadratic jet of $f$ can also make $U_{\tau ik}=0$ there. We call these the tilted logarithmic coordinates. The coordinate changes used to evaluate a jet are held fixed while that jet is differentiated.

<a id="stmt-fiber-blocks"></a>
<div id="fiber:blocks" class="lemma">

<a id="fiber:blockAi"></a>
<a id="fiber:blockL"></a>
**Lemma 11** (The six curvature blocks). *In tilted logarithmic coordinates, the tensor <a href="#fiber:curvature-convention" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:curvature-convention">[fiber:curvature-convention]</a> has the following blocks: <a id="fiber:blockA"></a><a id="fiber:blockW"></a><a id="fiber:blockspin"></a><a id="fiber:blockK"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 A:=C_{0\bar0 0\bar0}
   &=P^2P_{\tau\tau}-|\partial P|_h^2,
 \label{fiber:blockA}\\
 A_i:=C_{i\bar0 0\bar0}
   &=P\bigl(\partial_iP_\tau-
       (\mathcal N h^{-1}\partial P)_i\bigr),
 \label{fiber:blockAi}\\
 W_{i\bar j}:=C_{i\bar j0\bar0}
   &=P\bigl(\partial_i\partial_{\bar j}\log P
       +P_\tau\mathcal N_{i\bar j}
       -P(\mathcal N h^{-1}\mathcal N)_{i\bar j}\bigr),
 \label{fiber:blockW}\\
 C_{i\bar0 k\bar0}&=(\nabla_h^{2,0}P)_{ik},
 \label{fiber:blockspin}\\
 K_{ik\bar j}:=C_{i\bar j k\bar0}
   &=P\nabla_i^h\mathcal N_{k\bar j}
      +P_i\mathcal N_{k\bar j}+P_k\mathcal N_{i\bar j},
 \label{fiber:blockK}\\
 L_{i\bar j k\bar l}:=C_{i\bar j k\bar l}
   &=C^h_{i\bar j k\bar l}
      +P(\mathcal N_{i\bar j}\mathcal N_{k\bar l}
          +\mathcal N_{i\bar l}\mathcal N_{k\bar j}).
 \label{fiber:blockL}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $C^h$ and $\nabla^h$ are the curvature with sign <a href="#fiber:curvature-convention" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:curvature-convention">[fiber:curvature-convention]</a> and the Kähler connection of the fixed-$\tau$ spatial metric. Index positions in the products use the indicated Hermitian metric, with the usual conjugations.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fiber-blocks"><summary>Proof of stmt-fiber-blocks</summary>

<a id="fiber:cubics"></a>
*Proof.* In addition to the tilt, first choose spatial coordinates normal for $h$ at the point. On functions of $(s,\tau)$, physical coordinate derivatives act as $$D_i=\partial_i+P U_{\tau i}\partial_\tau,
 \qquad D_0=P\partial_\tau,$$ and their conjugates. At the point the complete list of cubic entries needed below is $$\label{fiber:cubics}
 \ell_{00\bar0}=PP_\tau,\quad
 \ell_{i0\bar0}=P_i,\quad
 \ell_{00\bar j}=P_{\bar j},\quad
 \ell_{i0\bar j}=P\mathcal N_{i\bar j},\quad
 \ell_{ik\bar0}=0,\quad \ell_{ik\bar j}=0.$$ For example, the identity $P^2\partial_\tau U_{\tau i}=P_i$, obtained by spatial differentiation of $U_{\tau\tau}=-1/P$, and its conjugate give the second and third entries. Directly differentiating the metric entries before evaluating gives $$\begin{aligned}
 D_0D_{\bar0}P&=P(P_\tau^2+PP_{\tau\tau}),\\
 D_iD_{\bar0}P&=P_iP_\tau+P\partial_iP_\tau,\\
 D_iD_{\bar j}P&=P_{i\bar j}+P\mathcal N_{i\bar j}P_\tau,\\
 D_iD_{\bar j}(PU_{\tau k})
   &=P_i\mathcal N_{k\bar j}+P_k\mathcal N_{i\bar j}
      +P\partial_i\mathcal N_{k\bar j},\\
 D_iD_{\bar j}(h_{k\bar l}+PU_{\tau k}U_{\tau\bar l})
   &=\partial_i\partial_{\bar j}h_{k\bar l}
      +P\mathcal N_{i\bar j}\mathcal N_{k\bar l}
      +P\mathcal N_{i\bar l}\mathcal N_{k\bar j}.
\end{aligned}$$ For completeness, the fourth derivative giving the spin block has a cancellation which is useful later. Put $a_i=U_{\tau i}$. Before evaluation, $$\ell_{ik\bar0}=Pa_{ik}+P_i a_k+P_k a_i+PP_\tau a_i a_k.$$ At the point $a_i=a_{ik}=0$, while $$(a_i)_\tau=P_i/P^2,\qquad
 (a_{ik})_\tau=P_{ik}/P^2-2P_iP_k/P^3.$$ Applying $D_{\bar0}=P\partial_\tau$ therefore gives $P_{ik}$: the two positive terms $P_iP_k/P$ cancel the negative doubled term.

Subtract the cubic contractions in <a href="#fiber:curvature-convention" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:curvature-convention">[fiber:curvature-convention]</a>. For A the vertical contraction is $PP_\tau^2$ and the spatial one is $|\partial P|_h^2$. For $A_i$ they are $P_iP_\tau$ and $P(\mathcal Nh^{-1}\partial P)_i$. For $W$ they are $P_iP_{\bar j}/P$ and $P^2(\mathcal Nh^{-1}\mathcal N)_{i\bar j}$. The first combines with $P_{i\bar j}$ to give $P\partial_i\partial_{\bar j}\log P$. For the purely spatial block the contraction is the one defining $C^h$. These calculations prove all formulas in normal spatial coordinates. Without spatial normalization, the last entry of <a href="#fiber:cubics" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:cubics">[fiber:cubics]</a> is $\partial_i h_{k\bar j}$; its contractions replace $P_{ik}$ by $\nabla^h_i\partial_kP$ and $\partial_i\mathcal N_{k\bar j}$ by $\nabla_i^h\mathcal N_{k\bar j}$. This also verifies the displayed tensorial formulas in general spatial coordinates. ◻

</details>

The six blocks give the complete tensor, but the desired sign concerns real sectional curvature. The next lemma identifies the matrix tests corresponding to real two-planes and records exactly how much of the diagonal curvature can absorb each mixed block.

<a id="fiber:ranknorm"></a>
We specify the norm convention needed to keep all subsequent constants independent of $n$. The norm of a covariant tensor is its multilinear operator norm on unit vectors in the stated fixed frame. The norm of a Hermitian test matrix is its Frobenius norm. Thus, for a Hermitian matrix $T$ of rank at most two and a covariant Hermitian form $Q$, $$\label{fiber:ranknorm}
 |Q:T|\le\sqrt2\|Q\|\,|T|.$$ Indeed diagonalize $T$; the sum of the absolute values of its at most two nonzero eigenvalues is at most $\sqrt2|T|$. The same decomposition bounds a curvature pairing $C(T,T)$ by $2\|C\||T|^2$. Contractions of cubic tensors through an inverse metric can instead be regarded as inner products of two covectors. Their operator bounds therefore involve no summation factor depending on the dimension.

<a id="stmt-fiber-ranktwo"></a>
<div id="fiber:ranktwo" class="lemma">

<a id="fiber:contraction"></a>
<a id="fiber:otherterms"></a>
<a id="fiber:reserve"></a>
<a id="fiber:spinabs"></a>
<a id="fiber:Aiabs"></a>
<a id="fiber:Kabs"></a>
<a id="fiber:retained"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Real planes and rank-two absorption). *If $C(H,H)\ge0$ for every Hermitian contravariant matrix $H$ of rank at most two, the underlying real sectional curvatures are nonpositive. Write the spatial, cross, and fiber diagonal blocks of $H$ as $T,b_\times,t$. The contraction has the terms $$\label{fiber:contraction}
 At^2+2t(W:T)+2W(b_\times,\bar b_\times)+L(T,T),$$ and its other terms have absolute value at most $$\label{fiber:otherterms}
 2|\nabla_h^{2,0}P(b_\times,b_\times)|
 +4|tA_i b_\times^i|+4|(K:T)b_\times|.$$ In particular the following quantitative criterion suffices. Suppose $A>0$, $W>0$, and a nonnegative quadratic form $\mathcal R(T)$ satisfies $$\begin{aligned}
 L(T,T)-\frac{|W:T|^2}{.98A}&\ge\mathcal R(T),
 \label{fiber:reserve}\\
 |\nabla_h^{2,0}P(b,b)|&\le\tfrac14 W(b,\bar b),
 \label{fiber:spinabs}\\
 |A_i b^i|&\le\eta\sqrt{A W(b,\bar b)},
 \label{fiber:Aiabs}\\
 |(K:T)b|&\le\eta\sqrt{\mathcal R(T)W(b,\bar b)},
 \label{fiber:Kabs}
\end{aligned}$$ for all such tests, where $\eta\le1/100$. Then $$\label{fiber:retained}
 C(H,H)\ge .01At^2+W(b_\times,\bar b_\times)
                         +\tfrac12\mathcal R(T).$$ All constants are independent of dimension.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fiber-ranktwo"><summary>Proof of stmt-fiber-ranktwo</summary>

*Proof.* For real vectors with $(1,0)$ parts $v,w$, set $H=i(v\otimes\bar w-w\otimes\bar v)$. This is Hermitian of rank at most two. Kähler symmetry gives $$C(H,H)=2\{C(v,\bar v,w,\bar w)
                   -\operatorname{Re}C(v,\bar w,v,\bar w)\}.$$ Expand $R(v+\bar v,w+\bar w,w+\bar w,v+\bar v)$. The only four nonzero terms are the two alternating type patterns and their conjugates. In the convention in which a real vector with complex components $v$ has squared length $g_{i\bar j}v^i\bar v^j$, the complex bilinear extension of the real metric on a holomorphic and an antiholomorphic vector is one half the Hermitian pairing. Accordingly the real curvature numerator is $-\tfrac12 C(H,H)$. This proves the sign assertion. Expanding a general block matrix $H$ gives <a href="#fiber:contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:contraction">[fiber:contraction]</a>–<a href="#fiber:otherterms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:otherterms">[fiber:otherterms]</a>; each mixed conjugate pair accounts for its factor two or four. A principal block $T$ has rank at most two.

For the quantitative assertion use $2t(W:T)\ge-.98At^2-|W:T|^2/(.98A)$. The remaining diagonal coefficient is .02A. The spin term costs at most $W/2$. The other two terms obey $$4\eta |t|\sqrt{AW}\le .01At^2+400\eta^2W,
 \qquad
 4\eta\sqrt{\mathcal R W}\le\tfrac12\mathcal R+8\eta^2W.$$ Since $1.5-408\eta^2\ge1$ when $\eta\le1/100$, <a href="#fiber:retained" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:retained">[fiber:retained]</a> follows. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-fiber-perturbation"></a>
<div id="fiber:perturbation" class="lemma">

<a id="fiber:perturbformula"></a>
<a id="fiber:perturbbound"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Exact perturbation identity). *In any fixed holomorphic coordinates let $H$ denote the cubic jet viewed as a map from two tangent inputs to a covector, and write $H=\Gamma g$. For a perturbation with $g+\Delta g>0$, the change of the cubic contraction $Hg^{-1}H^*$ is exactly $$\label{fiber:perturbformula}
 \Gamma\Delta H^*+\Delta H\Gamma^*-\Gamma\Delta g\Gamma^*
 +(\Delta H-\Gamma\Delta g)(g+\Delta g)^{-1}
                 (\Delta H-\Gamma\Delta g)^*.$$ Consequently, if $\|g^{-1}\Delta g\|$ in metric-unit axes is at most one half, the inverse of the perturbed metric is at most twice that of $g$, and $$\begin{aligned}
\label{fiber:perturbbound}
 \|\Delta C\|\le {}&\|\Delta j^4\ell\|
       +2\|\Gamma\|\|\Delta H\|
       +\|\Gamma\|^2\|\Delta g\|\\
 &+2\|g^{-1}\|
       (\|\Delta H\|+\|\Gamma\|\|\Delta g\|)^2.
 \notag
\end{aligned}$$ The same bound, with an absolute factor, controls rank-two contractions.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fiber-perturbation"><summary>Proof of stmt-fiber-perturbation</summary>

*Proof.* Set $Q=\Delta H-\Gamma\Delta g$. Then $H+\Delta H=\Gamma(g+\Delta g)+Q$. Multiply out the corresponding cubic contraction and subtract $\Gamma g\Gamma^*$. The two cross terms and $\Gamma\Delta g\Gamma^*$ combine to give the first three terms in <a href="#fiber:perturbformula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:perturbformula">[fiber:perturbformula]</a>. Taking operator norms gives <a href="#fiber:perturbbound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:perturbbound">[fiber:perturbbound]</a>. The inverse comparison is the elementary spectral estimate for $I+g^{-1/2}\Delta g g^{-1/2}$; the final statement uses <a href="#fiber:ranknorm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:ranknorm">[fiber:ranknorm]</a> twice. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:quiet"></a>
### A spatially varying quiet fiber profile

We now choose a radial fiber profile that tolerates spatial variation of its parameters. Its steep part gives the curvature margins needed near the testing disks; its logarithmic tail keeps the whole affine fiber available. After establishing the scalar estimates, we use the six-block formulas to prove a uniform curvature criterion and then show that it survives localized shear terms.

Fix $b_0\ge1000$ and $K_0\ge1$; increasing $K_0$ later is allowed. Define a positive function $u$ of $\tau>0$ by <a id="quiet:u"></a>$$\label{quiet:u}
 u=\tau\exp\left(\int_0^\tau
       \frac{(1+v)^{-1/16}-1}{v}\,dv\right).$$ It satisfies $u/\tau\to1$ at zero and $d\log u/d\log\tau=(1+\tau)^{-1/16}$. It is increasing, is comparable to $\tau$ on $(0,1]$, and tends to a finite positive limit at infinity: the last assertion follows by integrating $(1+\tau)^{-1/16}d\tau/\tau$ from 1 to infinity. More specifically, convexity gives $(1+v)^{-1/16}\ge1-v/16$, whence $e^{-1/16}\tau\le u\le\tau$ for $0<\tau\le1$. For a real parameter $E$, called the severity, set <a id="quiet:q"></a>$$\label{quiet:q}
 q=q_0(x,E)=(1+e^E u)^{b_0}+K_0^{-1}\log(1+\tau),
 \qquad x=\log\tau.$$ Constants in this section may depend on $b_0,K_0$, but are independent of the spatial dimension. A lower threshold for $E$ will always be chosen sufficiently large for these constants. The primitive $F$ has the normalization <a href="#fiber:F" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:F">[fiber:F]</a>. Because $q-1=O_E(\tau)$ at zero, this normalization exists. Because $q$ grows only linearly in $x$ at the right end, $F\to+\infty$ there.

<a id="stmt-quiet-scalar"></a>
<div id="quiet:scalar" class="lemma">

<a id="quiet:derivatives"></a>
<a id="quiet:Fderivatives"></a>
<a id="quiet:difference"></a>
<a id="quiet:intermediate"></a>
<a id="quiet:tail"></a>
<a id="quiet:middle-slope"></a>
<a id="quiet:axis-slope"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Scalar estimates for the profile). *Put $$q_a=(1+e^Eu)^{b_0},\quad r=r_q=q_x/q,\quad
 D=D_q=(q_x+q_{xx})/q=r(1+r)+r_x,\quad e_0=q_E/q.$$ For all sufficiently large $E$ and all $\tau>0$, <a id="quiet:slopes"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 q\ge1,\quad q_x>0,\quad
 D\ge r(r+\tfrac12),\quad q_{xx}/q_x\ge-\tfrac1{16},
 \label{quiet:slopes}\\
 0<r\le C,\quad |e_0|+|q_{EE}/q|\le C,
 \quad |r_E|\le Cr.
 \label{quiet:derivatives}
\end{gathered}$$ For $\tau\le1$, the first two quantities in the middle of <a href="#quiet:derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:derivatives">[quiet:derivatives]</a> are also at most $Cr$. For $1\le j\le4$, $$\label{quiet:Fderivatives}
 |\partial_E^jF|+|\partial_E^j\bar F|\le C,
 \qquad \bar F(\tau,E)=\tau^{-1}\int_0^\tau F(\log v,E)\,dv.$$ For $0\le j\le4$ and $\tau\ge e^{-E/4}$, $$\label{quiet:difference}
 |\partial_E^j(F-\bar F)|\le C e^{-E/4},\qquad
 |\partial_E^j(F-\bar F)|\le Cr.$$ The following additional estimates will be used: $$\begin{aligned}
 q_x&\ge e^E &&(e^{-E/4}\le\tau\le e^E),
 \label{quiet:intermediate}\\
 q&\le C(e^{b_0E}+\log(1+\tau)),\qquad
 q_x\ge c(1+e^{b_0E}(1+\tau)^{-1/16}) &&(\tau\ge1),
 \label{quiet:tail}\\
 r&\ge .9 b_0(1+\tau)^{-1/16}
              &&(1\le\tau\le e^{3b_0E}),
 \label{quiet:middle-slope}\\
 q&\le C,\quad q-1\le Ce^E\tau,\quad
 r\asymp e^E\tau,\quad D\asymp e^E\tau
              &&(0<\tau\le2e^{-E}).
 \label{quiet:axis-slope}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular $q_x\ge c$ for $\tau\ge e^{-E}$, and $q^{-1}\le Cr$ for $\tau\ge2e^{-E}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-quiet-scalar"><summary>Proof of stmt-quiet-scalar</summary>

*Proof.* Write $a=(1+\tau)^{-1/16}$, $z=e^Eu$, and $q_b=K_0^{-1}\log(1+\tau)$. The logarithmic slopes of the two summands of $q$ are $$p=\frac{(q_a)_x}{q_a}=b_0a\frac z{1+z},\qquad
 d=\frac{(q_b)_x}{q_b}
     =\frac{\tau}{(1+\tau)\log(1+\tau)}.$$ They satisfy $$p_x/p\ge-\tfrac1{16},\qquad
 d_x/d=\frac1{1+\tau}
       -\frac{\tau}{(1+\tau)\log(1+\tau)}\ge-\tfrac12.$$ For the second inequality use $\log(1+\tau)\ge2\tau/(2+\tau)$, obtained by differentiation from equality at zero. The derivative of a weighted mean of logarithmic slopes is the weighted mean of their derivatives plus their nonnegative variance. Hence $r_x\ge-r/2$, proving the first bound for $D$. Also $(q_a)_{xx}/(q_a)_x=p+p_x/p\ge-1/16$ and $(q_b)_{xx}/(q_b)_x=(1+\tau)^{-1}>0$. Taking the weighted mean with weights $(q_a)_x,(q_b)_x$ gives the other slope bound.

<a id="quiet:reciprocal"></a>
Every positive $E$ derivative of $q_a$ of order at most four is bounded by $Cq_a\min(1,z)$. Differentiating $q^{-1}$ thus gives $$\label{quiet:reciprocal}
 |\partial_E^j(q^{-1})|
       \le C\frac{q_a}{q^2}\min(1,z),\qquad 1\le j\le4.$$ Likewise $(q_a)_x$ and its $E$ derivative have ratio bounded in absolute value by a profile constant, so $|r_E|\le Cr$. Since $a\ge2^{-1/16}$ for $\tau\le1$, comparison with $p$ gives $e_0,q_{EE}/q\le Cr$ there. The remaining bounds in <a href="#quiet:derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:derivatives">[quiet:derivatives]</a> follow directly from the displayed formulas.

Differentiate the normalized integral for $F$. For $x\le0$, use $d x=d\log z/a$, where $a\ge2^{-1/16}$, in <a href="#quiet:reciprocal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:reciprocal">[quiet:reciprocal]</a>; the resulting absolute integral is at most $$C\int_0^\infty\frac{\min(1,z)}{(1+z)^{b_0}}\,\frac{dz}{z}<\infty.$$ For $x\ge0$, put $M=e^{b_0E}$. The positive lower and upper bounds on $u$ for $\tau\ge1$ imply $cM\le q_a\le CM$ and $q\ge cM+x/K_0$. The bound in <a href="#quiet:reciprocal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:reciprocal">[quiet:reciprocal]</a> is therefore at most $CM/(cM+x/K_0)^2$, whose integral over $[0,\infty)$ is uniformly bounded. This proves <a href="#quiet:Fderivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Fderivatives">[quiet:Fderivatives]</a>, also for the average by integration.

Integration by parts gives, including for $j=0$, <a id="quiet:averageidentity"></a>$$\label{quiet:averageidentity}
 \partial_E^j(F-\bar F)
   =\int_0^1\partial_E^j(q^{-1})(\log\tau+\log t,E)\,dt.$$ If $\tau\ge e^{-E/4}$, split at $t=e^{-E/4}$. On the first part the integrand is uniformly bounded. On the second part $\tau t\ge e^{-E/2}$, and $u(v)\ge c\min(v,1)$ implies $e^E u(\tau t)\ge c e^{E/2}$. There the integrand, also for $j=0$, is at most $C e^{-b_0E/2}$. This proves the first bound in <a href="#quiet:difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:difference">[quiet:difference]</a>. For $\tau\ge2$, split instead at $t=\tau^{-1/2}$. Monotonicity and the positive finite limit of $u$, together with $\log(1+\sqrt\tau)\ge\tfrac12\log(1+\tau)$, give $q(\log\sqrt\tau,E)\ge c q(\log\tau,E)$. Thus the integral is at most $C(\tau^{-1/2}+q^{-1})$. The estimates $q\le C(M+\log\tau)$ and $q_x\ge c(1+M\tau^{-1/16})$ show that $q\tau^{-1/2}\le Cq_x$ and $1\le Cq_x$; hence this last bound is at most $Cr$. On $[e^{-E/4},2]$ the explicit formula for $p$ gives a positive lower bound on $r$. This completes <a href="#quiet:difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:difference">[quiet:difference]</a>.

The estimates <a href="#quiet:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:tail">[quiet:tail]</a> follow from the same two summands of $q$. For $1\le\tau\le e^{3b_0E}$, the logarithmic summand divided by $q_a$ tends uniformly to zero as the lower threshold on $E$ increases; also $z/(1+z)\to1$ uniformly. This proves <a href="#quiet:middle-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:middle-slope">[quiet:middle-slope]</a>. On $[e^{-E/4},e^E]$ one has $z\ge c e^{3E/4}$ and $a\ge c e^{-E/16}$, so $$q_x\ge(q_a)_x=b_0az(1+z)^{b_0-1}
     \ge c\exp((3b_0/4-1/16)E)\ge e^E.$$ Finally, on $(0,2e^{-E}]$, the quantity $z$ is comparable to $e^E\tau$ and remains bounded by a profile constant. The formulas for the first two $x$ derivatives of $q$ then give <a href="#quiet:axis-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:axis-slope">[quiet:axis-slope]</a>. Between $2e^{-E}$ and 1 the same formulas give $q_x\ge c$; for $\tau\ge1$, use <a href="#quiet:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:tail">[quiet:tail]</a>. These statements also prove the two final consequences. ◻

</details>

The scalar estimates make two tasks compatible: the spatial Schur metric remains positive, and the fiber curvature absorbs the trace couplings in a real-plane test. The following criterion states the spatial hypotheses needed for both tasks. The word *quiet* refers to the smallness of the severity derivatives in the metric of the twist.

<a id="stmt-quiet-profile"></a>
<div id="quiet:profile" class="proposition">

**Proposition 15** (Quiet profile criterion). *There exist positive constants $\epsilon,\mu$ and a lower threshold $E_{\min}$, depending on the fixed profile constants but not on $n$, with the following property. Let $E$ be a smooth spatial function and use $q=q_0(x,E(s))$ with constant center and no shear. At a spatial point suppose, in some fixed holomorphic affine coordinates, that:*

1.  *$G\ge V>0$. The operator norms of $V,G$, their inverses, their first and second metric jets, and all derivatives of $E$ of orders one through four are at most $e^{\epsilon E}$.*

2.  *For every Hermitian contravariant matrix $T$ of rank at most two, $C^V(T,T)\ge e^{-\epsilon E}|T|^2$, with the norm in those affine coordinates.*

3.  *In $G$-unit axes, $$|\partial E|_G^2\le\mu,\qquad
     \|\partial\bar\partial E\|_G\le\mu,\qquad
     \|\nabla_h^{2,0}E\|_G\le\mu
     \quad\hbox{for every }\tau>0.$$*

<a id="quiet:schur"></a>
<a id="quiet:margins"></a>
*If $E\ge E_{\min}$, the resulting metric is positive for every $y$ over that spatial point and has nonpositive real sectional curvature there. Uniformly in $\tau$, $$\label{quiet:schur}
 \mathcal N=(1+O(\mu))G,\qquad
 h\asymp V+\tau G.$$ More precisely, off the fiber axis the rank-two curvature calculation retains fixed positive multiples of $$\label{quiet:margins}
 At^2+W(b_\times,\bar b_\times)+P|T|_{\mathcal N}^2
 +\boldsymbol{1}_{\{\tau\le e^{-E/4}\}}
                          e^{-\epsilon E}|T|^2.$$ Here the fiber blocks use the tilted logarithmic coordinates, $|T|_{\mathcal N}$ is its Frobenius norm in $\mathcal N$-unit spatial axes, and $\boldsymbol{1}$ denotes the indicated indicator. The meaning of the final hypothesis is noncircular: positivity of $h$ follows from the preceding hypotheses and does not use $\nabla_h^{2,0}E$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-quiet-profile"><summary>Proof of stmt-quiet-profile</summary>

<a id="quiet:N"></a>
*Proof.* Differentiating $U_\tau=\psi-F$ gives $$\label{quiet:N}
 \mathcal N=G-F_E\partial\bar\partial E
               -F_{EE}\partial E\otimes\bar\partial E,
 \qquad h=V+\tau\bar{\mathcal N}.$$ The bar denotes averaging from 0 to $\tau$. The bounded derivatives in <a href="#quiet:Fderivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Fderivatives">[quiet:Fderivatives]</a> give $(1-C\mu)G\le\mathcal N,\bar{\mathcal N}\le(1+C\mu)G$. Choose $\mu$ small enough that $C\mu<1/10$. This proves positivity and <a href="#quiet:schur" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:schur">[quiet:schur]</a>.

We will repeatedly use finite-jet estimates of the following precise kind. Differentiating <a href="#quiet:N" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:N">[quiet:N]</a> at most twice spatially gives sums with at most four derivatives of $E$ and at most four $E$ derivatives of $F$ or $\bar F$. Each summand is a product of a fixed number of the assumed jets. The formulas for the inverse, connection, and curvature add a fixed number of inverse metric factors. Consequently there is an absolute finite constant $C_*$, independent of dimension, such that these estimates cost at most $C e^{C_*\epsilon E}$, with the factors $1+\tau$ or $(1+\tau)^{-1}$ displayed explicitly below. This conclusion follows by the product rule and $h^{-1}\le C e^{\epsilon E}(1+\tau)^{-1}I$; it uses operator norms, not sums of tensor entries. The same observation applies after normalizing spatial axes by $\mathcal N$, because $e^{-\epsilon E}I/C\le\mathcal N\le C e^{\epsilon E}I$. Enlarge $C_*$ once to cover these finitely many products, and choose $\epsilon>0$ so small that $C_*\epsilon<1/100$. Any profile-dependent multiplicative constants are absorbed by raising $E_{\min}$.

<a id="quiet:Y"></a>
<a id="quiet:Ybounds"></a>
In $\mathcal N$-unit axes define $$\label{quiet:Y}
 \mathbf Y=I-\tau\mathcal N^{1/2}h^{-1}\mathcal N^{1/2}.$$ The displayed formula indicates the normalization; equivalently it is $I-\tau h^{-1}$ after $\mathcal N=I$ has been imposed in the fixed axes at the point. We claim $$\label{quiet:Ybounds}
 \|\mathbf Y\|\le r/20\quad(\tau\ge2e^{-E}),\qquad
 \mathbf Y=I+O(e^{-(1-C_*\epsilon)E})
                         \quad(\tau\le2e^{-E}).$$ Always $\|\mathbf Y\|\le1+C\mu$, because $h\ge\tau\bar{\mathcal N}$ and $\bar{\mathcal N}$ is close to $\mathcal N$. On $[2e^{-E},1]$, the explicit slope $p$ in the scalar lemma yields $r\ge .35b_0$ for all sufficiently large $E$: indeed $u\ge e^{-1/16}\tau$, $z\ge2e^{-1/16}$, $a\ge2^{-1/16}$, and $q_a/q\ge(1+\log2/K_0)^{-1}\ge(1+\log2)^{-1}$. The product of these three lower bounds in $p(q_a/q)=b_0a[z/(1+z)](q_a/q)$ exceeds $.35b_0$. This proves the first bound on that interval. For $\tau\ge1$, <a href="#quiet:difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:difference">[quiet:difference]</a> gives $\|\bar{\mathcal N}-\mathcal N\|_{\mathcal N}\le C\mu r$. Set $h_0=V+\tau\mathcal N$. The inverse identity and $h,h_0\ge c\tau\mathcal N$ show that replacing $h$ by $h_0$ in <a href="#quiet:Y" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Y">[quiet:Y]</a> costs at most $C\mu r$. In the normalized axes $V\le\gamma I$, where $\gamma=(1-C\mu)^{-1}$, and hence $$\|I-\tau h_0^{-1}\|\le\frac\gamma{\gamma+\tau}.$$ This is at most $r/40$ on $[1,e^{3b_0E}]$, by <a href="#quiet:middle-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:middle-slope">[quiet:middle-slope]</a> and $b_0\ge1000$. For $\tau>e^{3b_0E}$, use $r\ge c/q$ and $q/\tau\le C(e^{b_0E}+\log(1+\tau))/\tau$; the supremum of the last expression on this range tends to zero with the lower threshold on $E$. Thus the same estimate holds there. Decreasing $\mu$ pays the inverse-identity error. Finally, if $\tau\le2e^{-E}$, then $h\ge V$ and the raw size bounds give $\tau\|\mathcal N^{1/2}h^{-1}\mathcal N^{1/2}\|
 \le C\tau e^{C_*\epsilon E}$, proving the second bound.

<a id="quiet:Wformula"></a>
<a id="quiet:Wbounds"></a>
Since $P_\tau=q(1+r)$ and $P^2P_{\tau\tau}=Pq^2D$, the curvature blocks in Lemma <a href="#fiber:blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fiber:blocks">11</a> become <a id="quiet:spinformula"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \frac W{Pq}&=rI+\mathbf Y+q^{-1}\partial\bar\partial\log q,
 \label{quiet:Wformula}\\
 P^{-1}\nabla_h^{2,0}P
     &=e_0\nabla_h^{2,0}E+(q_{EE}/q)\partial E\otimes\partial E.
 \label{quiet:spinformula}
\end{aligned}$$ The final term in <a href="#quiet:Wformula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Wformula">[quiet:Wformula]</a> and the spin form divided by $Pq$ have norm at most $C\mu r$ when $\tau\ge2e^{-E}$, and at most $C\mu$ on the remaining interval. This follows from <a href="#quiet:derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:derivatives">[quiet:derivatives]</a>, the three smallness assumptions on $E$, and $q^{-1}\le Cr$ away from the exceptional interval. After decreasing $\mu$, $$\label{quiet:Wbounds}
 W\asymp Pqr\,\mathcal N\quad(\tau\ge2e^{-E}),\qquad
 W\asymp Pq(1+r)\,\mathcal N\quad(\tau\le2e^{-E}),$$ and the spin inequality <a href="#fiber:spinabs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:spinabs">[fiber:spinabs]</a> holds.

<a id="quiet:Abounds"></a>
For the fiber diagonal term, $$\frac{|\partial P|_h^2}{Pq^2D}
 \le C\mu\frac{e_0^2}{qD}\le C\mu.$$ Here $h\ge c\tau G$; the final scalar quotient is bounded using $e_0\le Cr$ for $\tau\le1$, and $qD\ge cqr=cq_x\ge c$ for $\tau\ge1$. Thus $$\label{quiet:Abounds}
 A=(1+O(\mu))Pq^2D>0.$$ In the same normalized axes the cancellation in <a href="#fiber:blockAi" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:blockAi">[fiber:blockAi]</a> gives $$|A_{\cdot}|_{\mathcal N}
 \le CPq\sqrt\mu\bigl(|e_0|(r+\|\mathbf Y\|)+|r_E|\bigr)
 \le CPq\sqrt\mu\,r.$$ The last inequality uses $e_0\le Cr$ on the exceptional interval. Combining with <a href="#quiet:Wbounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Wbounds">[quiet:Wbounds]</a> and <a href="#quiet:Abounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Abounds">[quiet:Abounds]</a>, the squared ratio to the dual norm determined by $AW$ is at most $C\mu$. Consequently <a href="#fiber:Aiabs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:Aiabs">[fiber:Aiabs]</a> holds with any fixed desired small $\eta>0$ after decreasing $\mu$.

<a id="quiet:tracecost"></a>
We next pay the trace coupling. Outside the exceptional interval, <a href="#quiet:Ybounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Ybounds">[quiet:Ybounds]</a> and <a href="#quiet:Wformula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Wformula">[quiet:Wformula]</a> imply $$|W:T|\le Pqr\left(|\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal N}T|
                       +\frac{\sqrt2}{16}|T|_{\mathcal N}\right).$$ By $D\ge r^2$, its cost against .98A is at most $$\label{quiet:tracecost}
 \frac P{.98(1-C\mu)}
 \left(|\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal N}T|
                       +\frac{\sqrt2}{16}|T|_{\mathcal N}\right)^2.$$ For $0\le s\le\sqrt2$, $(s+\sqrt2/16)^2/(1+s^2)<.8$; this can also be checked by differentiating, since its maximum on this interval occurs at the right endpoint. Because $T$ has rank at most two, decreasing $\mu$ therefore makes <a href="#quiet:tracecost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:tracecost">[quiet:tracecost]</a> at most $.85P((\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal N}T)^2+|T|_{\mathcal N}^2)$. These are exactly the positive symmetrized terms in <a href="#fiber:blockL" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:blockL">[fiber:blockL]</a>. On $\tau\le2e^{-E}$, the bounds $D\ge c e^E\tau$, $q,r\le C$, and <a href="#quiet:Wbounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Wbounds">[quiet:Wbounds]</a> instead give a trace cost at most $Ce^{-E}|T|_{\mathcal N}^2$.

<a id="quiet:Rreserve"></a>
To estimate the remaining $C^h$, note from <a href="#quiet:N" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:N">[quiet:N]</a> that the difference $h-V=\tau\bar{\mathcal N}$ and its first two jets are at most $C\tau e^{C_*\epsilon E}$. On $\tau\le e^{-E/4}$, the curvature formula or Lemma <a href="#fiber:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fiber:perturbation">13</a> consequently gives $$|C^h(T,T)-C^V(T,T)|
       \le C\tau e^{C_*\epsilon E}|T|^2
       \le\tfrac12e^{-\epsilon E}|T|^2.$$ In the complementary range, the inverse bound and the jet bounds give $$|C^h(T,T)|\le C(1+\tau)e^{C_*\epsilon E}|T|^2.$$ This is arbitrarily small compared with $P|T|_{\mathcal N}^2$: indeed, for $\tau\ge e^{-E/4}$, $\tau q_a/(1+\tau)\ge e^{2E}$ after increasing the threshold on $E$, by the explicit power $b_0\ge1000$; normalization of $T$ costs only $e^{C_*\epsilon E}$. On the exceptional interval, its trace cost $Ce^{-E}|T|_{\mathcal N}^2$ is likewise negligible compared with $e^{-\epsilon E}|T|^2$. We have thus obtained <a href="#fiber:reserve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:reserve">[fiber:reserve]</a> with $$\label{quiet:Rreserve}
 \mathcal R(T)\ge cP|T|_{\mathcal N}^2
  +c\boldsymbol{1}_{\{\tau\le e^{-E/4}\}}
                          e^{-\epsilon E}|T|^2,$$ before paying the $K$ coupling.

For that coupling split $K$ into $P\nabla^h\mathcal N$ and the two terms containing $P_i=Pe_0E_i$. The latter have normalized size at most $CP|e_0|\sqrt\mu$; their squared dual $W$ norm after pairing with $T$ is at most $C\mu P|T|_{\mathcal N}^2$. To see this directly outside the exceptional interval, the scalar quotient to bound is $e_0^2/(qr)$; it is bounded by <a href="#quiet:derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:derivatives">[quiet:derivatives]</a> and <a href="#quiet:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:tail">[quiet:tail]</a>. On the exceptional interval replace $r$ in that denominator by $1+r$, which is easier. For $P\nabla^h\mathcal N$ use three ranges:

1.  If $\tau\le e^{-E/4}$, then $W\ge cPq\mathcal N$, and the raw jet bounds give squared dual $W$ cost at most $C\tau e^{C_*\epsilon E}|T|^2$, negligible compared with the second term of <a href="#quiet:Rreserve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Rreserve">[quiet:Rreserve]</a>.

2.  If $e^{-E/4}\le\tau\le e^E$, use $\|\nabla^h\mathcal N\|_{\mathcal N}\le Ce^{C_*\epsilon E}$. The squared dual cost is at most $CPe^{C_*\epsilon E}(qr)^{-1}|T|_{\mathcal N}^2$. By <a href="#quiet:intermediate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:intermediate">[quiet:intermediate]</a> this is negligible compared with the first term of <a href="#quiet:Rreserve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Rreserve">[quiet:Rreserve]</a>.

3.  If $\tau>e^E$, the identity $\nabla^h h=0$ permits $$\nabla^h\mathcal N
       =\nabla^h(\mathcal N-\bar{\mathcal N}-V/\tau).$$ Equation <a href="#quiet:difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:difference">[quiet:difference]</a>, differentiated spatially once, and the raw bounds give a normalized norm at most $C e^{-E/4+C_*\epsilon E}$. In making this estimate the connection has raw norm at most $Ce^{C_*\epsilon E}$, because $h$ has first jets at most $C(1+\tau)e^{C_*\epsilon E}$ and inverse norm at most $Ce^{C_*\epsilon E}/(1+\tau)$. Since $qr=q_x\ge c$, the squared dual $W$ cost is again negligible compared with $P|T|_{\mathcal N}^2$.

Fix $\eta\le1/100$ in Lemma <a href="#fiber:ranktwo" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fiber:ranktwo">12</a>. Choose $\mu$ small enough for the terms proportional to $\mu$, and then $E_{\min}$ large enough for the exponentially small terms, so that <a href="#fiber:Kabs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:Kabs">[fiber:Kabs]</a> holds. That lemma proves positivity of every rank-two contraction with the retained margins <a href="#quiet:margins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:margins">[quiet:margins]</a>, and hence nonpositive real sectional curvature. Smoothness and positivity at the axis follow from Lemma <a href="#fiber:metric" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fiber:metric">10</a>; curvature extends by continuity. ◻

</details>

To realize the disks of Proposition <a href="#grid:criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="grid:criterion">8</a>, we need to add a term linear in the fiber variable whose coefficient varies over the spatial base. A locally constant coefficient contributes only a pluriharmonic potential. The issue is therefore its derivative support, where the estimates below must hold for arbitrarily large fiber radius.

<a id="stmt-quiet-shear"></a>
<div id="quiet:shear" class="proposition">

<a id="quiet:shear-small"></a>
**Proposition 16** (Localized shear perturbations). *Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#quiet:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="quiet:profile">15</a>, suppose $E$ and the center are constant on the spatial region under consideration. Assume also that the first and pure second spatial derivatives of $\psi$, in the affine coordinates of that proposition, have norms at most $e^{\epsilon E}$. Add $$\ell_{\mathrm{sh}}=\operatorname{Re}(c_{\mathrm{sh}}(s)(y-\zeta)).$$ On the support of its coefficient’s derivatives suppose $\psi\ge-J$, where $J\ge0$, and $$\label{quiet:shear-small}
 \|j^{\le4}c_{\mathrm{sh}}\|\le e^{-C E-J/2}.$$ For a sufficiently large $C$ depending only on the profile and the constants in the quiet criterion, this perturbation preserves metric positivity and nonpositive real sectional curvature for all $y$. Moreover its metric is at least one half the unperturbed metric, and fixed fractions of the curvature margins remain. The choice of $C$ is independent of dimension.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-quiet-shear"><summary>Proof of stmt-quiet-shear</summary>

*Proof.* Where the coefficient is locally constant the added potential is pluriharmonic, so it changes no metric. It remains to estimate the derivative collar in <a href="#quiet:shear-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:shear-small">[quiet:shear-small]</a>. Since $E$ is constant, $P_i=0$, $\mathcal N=G$, and $h=V+\tau G$. The holomorphic tilt used in Lemma <a href="#fiber:blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fiber:blocks">11</a> has linear and quadratic coefficients bounded by $Ce^{\epsilon E}$, because $U_\tau=\psi-F$. Its constant term may and will be zero. Derivatives through order four of the exponential of this fixed quadratic polynomial at the point cost at most $Ce^{C\epsilon E}$, by the ordinary product rule. These are fixed-order multilinear estimates.

<a id="quiet:large-margin"></a>
First consider $\tau\ge e^{-E}$ in tilted logarithmic coordinates. The metric inverse has norm at most $Ce^{C\epsilon E}/\tau$. By <a href="#quiet:Abounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Abounds">[quiet:Abounds]</a>, <a href="#quiet:Wbounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Wbounds">[quiet:Wbounds]</a>, <a href="#quiet:Rreserve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:Rreserve">[quiet:Rreserve]</a>, and $q_x\ge c$ on this range, the retained curvature on a rank-two matrix $H$ is at least $$\label{quiet:large-margin}
 c\tau e^{-C_1E}|H|^2$$ in these fixed coordinates, for a fixed $C_1$. The cubic table <a href="#fiber:cubics" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:cubics">[fiber:cubics]</a> shows explicitly that the only possibly nonzero connection blocks are $$\Gamma^0_{00}=P_\tau,\qquad
 \Gamma^j_{i0}=P(h^{-1}G)^j_i,\qquad
 \Gamma^k_{ij}=(\Gamma^h)^k_{ij}.$$ Consequently their operator norm is at most $Ce^{C_1E}q$, since $r$ is bounded. Allowing a larger fixed power of $q$ in the following estimates would give the same result.

<a id="quiet:log-absorption"></a>
The inequality $q\ge1$ and the left normalization imply $F\le x$. Thus $$|y-\zeta|=e^{(F-\psi)/2}\le e^{-\psi/2}\sqrt\tau.$$ After the tilt, all shear jets needed for the metric and curvature, of orders two, three, and four, are therefore at most <a id="quiet:shear-large-jets"></a>$$\label{quiet:shear-large-jets}
 C\sqrt\tau\exp((-C+C_2)E),$$ where $e^{-J/2}e^{-\psi/2}\le1$ was used. For every fixed integer $r_1$, the scalar estimate <a href="#quiet:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:tail">[quiet:tail]</a>, also valid as an upper bound for small $\tau$, gives $$\label{quiet:log-absorption}
 \sup_{\tau\ge e^{-E}}\frac{q^{r_1}}{\sqrt\tau}
       \le C_{r_1}e^{C_{r_1}E}.$$ Indeed $q^{r_1}\le C_{r_1}(e^{r_1b_0E}
+\log(1+\tau)^{r_1})$; on $[e^{-E},1]$ use $\tau^{-1/2}\le e^{E/2}$, and on $[1,\infty)$ the quotient $\log(1+\tau)^{r_1}/\sqrt\tau$ is bounded.

For clarity, this bound controls the quadratic perturbation as well as the linear terms. Write $\delta=e^{-CE}$ and absorb all other fixed $E$ powers into $e^{cE}$. The relative metric error is bounded by $\delta e^{cE}/\sqrt\tau$. Each linear curvature error from <a href="#fiber:perturbbound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:perturbbound">[fiber:perturbbound]</a>, divided by <a href="#quiet:large-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:large-margin">[quiet:large-margin]</a>, is bounded by $\delta e^{cE}q^{r_1}/\sqrt\tau$ for some fixed $r_1$. The quadratic contraction error before division by that margin is at most $\delta^2e^{cE}q^{2r_1}$; after division its bound is $$\delta^2e^{c'E}\left(\frac{q^{r_1}}{\sqrt\tau}\right)^2.$$ Equation <a href="#quiet:log-absorption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:log-absorption">[quiet:log-absorption]</a> makes all these quantities arbitrarily small by a single sufficiently large $C$. It follows that the metric is at least half its old value and that the curvature errors are smaller than a fixed fraction of the retained margin, uniformly on this whole fiber range.

<a id="quiet:small-z"></a>
For $0<\tau\le e^{-E}$, exponentiate the tilted logarithmic coordinate and use the affine fiber coordinate with scale $e^{-(\psi(s_0)+E)/2}$ at the fixed spatial point $s_0$. Denote its value by $z$. The zero constant term of the tilt and <a href="#quiet:axis-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:axis-slope">[quiet:axis-slope]</a> give $$\label{quiet:small-z}
 |z|^2=e^{E+F}\asymp e^E\tau.$$ For example, $|F-x|\le Ce^E\tau$ follows by integration of $(q^{-1}-1)d\tau/\tau$ on this range, and proves the comparison. The metric blocks in affine coordinates are $h$ and $P/|z|^2$. Both have inverse norm at most $e^{C_3E}$. The curvature tensor transforms tensorially: its fiber diagonal block divides by $|z|^4$, its $W$ block by $|z|^2$, and its spatial block is unchanged. Since $$A\ge c\tau^2e^E,\qquad W\ge c\tau G,\qquad
 \mathcal R(T)\ge c e^{-\epsilon E}|T|^2,$$ the retained rank-two curvature margin in affine coordinates is at least $e^{-C_3E}|H|^2$, after increasing $C_3$.

The connection in the new coordinates is also bounded by $e^{C_3E}$, without an apparent singularity at $z=0$. Indeed the logarithmic block $\Gamma^0_{00}=P_\tau$ becomes $(P_\tau-1)/z$, and $P_\tau-1=O(e^E\tau)$; thus it is bounded by $C\sqrt{e^E\tau}$. The mixed spatial block becomes $Ph^{-1}G/z$, bounded by $e^{C_3E}$ using <a href="#quiet:small-z" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="quiet:small-z">[quiet:small-z]</a>. The spatial-output, two-fiber-input block is zero because $P_i=0$, and the spatial connection is already bounded. These checks cover all cubic entries. The same bounds extend to zero by the smoothness proved in Lemma <a href="#fiber:metric" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fiber:metric">10</a>.

In these affine coordinates the shear jets of orders two through four are bounded by $$e^{-CE-J/2}e^{-(\psi(s_0)+E)/2}Ce^{C_4\epsilon E}
 \le C\exp(-(C+1/2-C_4\epsilon)E).$$ The estimate includes derivatives of the tilt and uses that $|z|$ is bounded on this range. Apply Lemma <a href="#fiber:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fiber:perturbation">13</a>, with the inverse metric, connection, and reciprocal margin bounded by fixed powers of $e^{C_3E}$. Increasing the same $C$ makes all relative metric errors and all curvature errors smaller than one half their respective retained bounds, including at the axis. The estimates overlap at $\tau=e^{-E}$, so they cover the entire fiber.

Finally, all exponents just used arise from derivatives of order at most four, a fixed finite number of products in <a href="#fiber:perturbformula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:perturbformula">[fiber:perturbformula]</a>, and operator norms of linear maps. Rank-two contractions introduce only the absolute constants in <a href="#fiber:ranknorm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fiber:ranknorm">[fiber:ranknorm]</a>. None of these operations counts spatial indices. This proves the asserted independence from $n$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:radial"></a>
### Radial spatial geometry and aligned profiles

The quiet criterion supplies the testing regions, where $G\ge V$. A center change requires a different arrangement: $V$ must be close to $\lambda G$ with a large scalar $\lambda$. This section constructs radial spatial metrics that can pass between these arrangements and proves the curvature estimate for the aligned regime. Here *aligned* means that their metric jets through order two are close to proportional, with the scalar frozen at the evaluation point.

All norms of covariant jets in this section are multilinear operator norms in the stated fixed affine coordinates. The norm of a Hermitian contravariant matrix is its Frobenius norm. Constants called absolute may depend on a fixed upper bound for the first two derivatives of $\kappa$ in $\log Y$, but not on the spatial complex dimension. This convention is essential when choosing the dimension only after the geometric constants.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### The radial clocks

Write $t=|s|^2$, $\nu=\log t$, and let $B$ and $\Psi$ be radial potentials. Put <a id="radial:definitions"></a>$$\label{radial:definitions}
 \begin{gathered}
 a=B'(t),\qquad a_\psi=\Psi'(t)=a/\lambda,\qquad
 u=\log a,\\
 Y=\frac{du}{d\nu},\qquad
 H=\frac{Y}{t a_\psi},\qquad
 p=\frac{d\log a_\psi}{du},\qquad
 \kappa=\frac{dY/d\nu}{Y(1+Y)}.
 \end{gathered}$$ Here a prime on a radial potential means differentiation in $t$. We use $u$ for this radial variable in this section and $u_s$ in Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">8</a>. We write $V=\partial\bar\partial B$ and $G_0=\partial\bar\partial\Psi$ for their Hermitian matrices.

These quantities separate the controls needed in the construction. The ratio $\lambda$ compares the transverse coefficients of $V$ and $G_0$, while $H/\lambda=Y/(ta)$ is the common scale of the spatial curvature components computed below. The evolution equations will give $d\Psi/du=1/H$ and $d\nu/du=1/Y$: large $H$ limits the change of the radial twist $\Psi$ during growth, and large $Y$ limits the spatial radius consumed. We use the first feature to prepare a center change and the second to keep each accelerated episode inside a finite radial interval. Capped growth then lets the spatial radius continue between successive tests.

<a id="stmt-radial-odes"></a>
<div id="radial:odes" class="lemma">

<a id="radial:kappa-assumptions"></a>
<a id="radial:clock-equations"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Radial evolution). *Suppose $a,a_\psi,Y>0$, $\lambda\ge1$, and $$\label{radial:kappa-assumptions}
 \frac1{1+Y}\le\kappa\le1.1,\qquad
 \frac{d\kappa}{d\log Y}\ge-\kappa.$$ Then $$\begin{aligned}
\label{radial:clock-equations}
 \frac{d\nu}{du}&=\frac1Y,&
 \frac{d\log Y}{du}&=\kappa(1+1/Y),&
 \frac{d\log\lambda}{du}&=1-p,\\
 \frac{d\Psi}{du}&=\frac1H,&
 \frac{d\log H}{du}&=\kappa(1+1/Y)-p-1/Y.\notag
\end{aligned}$$ If $Y\ge t$ initially, then $Y\ge t$ subsequently. With smooth bounded controls $p,\kappa$ on each finite $u$ interval, positive solutions have no blowup on that interval. A cap of the form $\kappa=C_\kappa/(1+Y)$, with fixed $C_\kappa\ge1$, has no blowup at finite $\nu$ for the prescribed bounded controls.*

<a id="radial:H-logY"></a>
*If $p=1$, then $$\label{radial:H-logY}
 \frac{d\log H}{d\log Y}=1-\frac1\kappa.$$ In particular, $p=\kappa=1$ holds both $\lambda$ and $H$ constant. If $\kappa\ge1$ on an interval beginning at $u_0$, then the total increase of $\nu$ on that interval is at most $1/Y(u_0)$, even if its finite $u$ length is arbitrarily large.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-radial-odes"><summary>Proof of stmt-radial-odes</summary>

*Proof.* The first three identities follow by changing the independent variable from $\nu$ to $u$. Since $d\Psi/d\nu=t a_\psi$ and $H=Y/(t a_\psi)$, the fourth follows as well. Differentiating $\log H=\log Y-\nu-\log a_\psi$ gives the fifth. The lower bound on $\kappa$ implies $$\frac{d\log(Y/t)}{d\nu}=\kappa(1+Y)-1\ge0.$$ In $u$ time, $dY/du=\kappa(1+Y)\le1.1(1+Y)$, so $Y$ remains finite and positive on each finite interval. The remaining variables are obtained by integrating the displayed equations there. In a cap, $dY/d\nu=C_\kappa Y$, which gives the asserted global $\nu$ continuation. Dividing the last equation of <a href="#radial:clock-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:clock-equations">[radial:clock-equations]</a>, with $p=1$, by the second proves <a href="#radial:H-logY" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:H-logY">[radial:H-logY]</a>. Finally, $\kappa\ge1$ gives $Y(u)\ge Y(u_0)e^{u-u_0}$, and integration of $d\nu/du=1/Y$ gives the last assertion. ◻

</details>

The inequalities in <a href="#radial:kappa-assumptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:kappa-assumptions">[radial:kappa-assumptions]</a> allow the smooth changes used later. For example, to decrease $\kappa$ between two fixed positive values, make the change over a sufficiently long fixed interval in $\log Y$; then $d\log\kappa/d\log Y\ge-1$. To pass from $\kappa=1$ to a cap, choose a smooth function $K(Y)$ which initially equals $1+Y$, has $0\le K'\le1$, and eventually is constant, and put $\kappa=K(Y)/(1+Y)$. The change can be made on a fixed increasing-factor interval of $Y$, so its first two $\log Y$ derivatives are bounded. Moreover $1\le K\le1+Y$ and $$Y\kappa_Y+\kappa
 =\frac{K+Y(1+Y)K'}{(1+Y)^2}\ge0.$$ Thus all inequalities persist, and $H$ is nonincreasing during this change when $p=1$.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Curvature and coordinate bounds

<a id="stmt-radial-curvature"></a>
<div id="radial:curvature" class="lemma">

<a id="radial:rank-reserve"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Radial curvature). *Under <a href="#radial:kappa-assumptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:kappa-assumptions">[radial:kappa-assumptions]</a>, the negative Hermitian curvature tensor $C^V=-R^V$ satisfies $$\label{radial:rank-reserve}
 C^V(T,T)\ge c\,\kappa\frac{H}{\lambda}|T|_V^2
 \qquad\text{if }T=T^*,\quad\operatorname{rank}T\le2,$$ where $c>0$ is absolute. Here the subscript $V$ means that the matrix is expressed in $V$-unit axes.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-radial-curvature"><summary>Proof of stmt-radial-curvature</summary>

<a id="radial:curvature-components"></a>
*Proof.* At $s=(\sqrt t,0,\ldots,0)$ the metric has entries $$V_{i\bar j}=a\delta_{ij}+a'\bar s_i s_j,$$ and its radial and transverse eigenvalues are $a(1+Y)$ and $a$. The unitary symmetry in the transverse variables and the Kähler symmetries leave only a transverse block, a radial–transverse bisectional block, and a pure radial entry. After removing the common factor $Y/(ta)=H/\lambda$, their respective values in $V$-unit axes are $$\label{radial:curvature-components}
 \delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{kj},\qquad
 \kappa\delta_{kl},\qquad
 D=\kappa\bigl(1+\kappa+Y\kappa_Y\bigr).$$ For completeness, the transverse coefficient is $a'/a^2=Y/(ta)$. The radial–transverse coefficient is $$\frac{dY/d\nu}{t a(1+Y)}=\kappa\frac{Y}{ta}.$$ For the pure radial entry, differentiate the logarithm of the radial metric along the radial complex line. Since $$\frac{d}{d\nu}\log\bigl(a(1+Y)\bigr)=Y(1+\kappa),$$ its second $\nu$ derivative divided by $ta(1+Y)$ is exactly the last expression in <a href="#radial:curvature-components" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:curvature-components">[radial:curvature-components]</a> times $Y/(ta)$. These calculations also follow directly by subtracting the cubic contractions in the Kähler curvature formula.

Write a Hermitian test matrix in radial/transverse blocks as $$T=\begin{pmatrix}r&b^*\\ b&S\end{pmatrix}.$$ The contraction after removing $Y/(ta)$ is $$Dr^2+2\kappa r\operatorname{tr}S+2\kappa|b|^2
       +(\operatorname{tr}S)^2+|S|^2.$$ The assumption on $\kappa_Y$ gives $D\ge\kappa$. Absorb the cross term with $0.9Dr^2$. Its cost is at most $$\frac{\kappa^2}{0.9D}(\operatorname{tr}S)^2
 \le\frac{1.1}{0.9}(\operatorname{tr}S)^2.$$ Since $\operatorname{rank}S\le2$, $|\operatorname{tr}S|\le\sqrt2|S|$. The transverse reserve is therefore at least $5|S|^2/9$, in addition to $0.1Dr^2$ and $2\kappa|b|^2$. As $\kappa\le1.1$, these bound an absolute positive multiple of $\kappa(r^2+2|b|^2+|S|^2)$. This proves <a href="#radial:rank-reserve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:rank-reserve">[radial:rank-reserve]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-radial-scaled"></a>
<div id="radial:scaled" class="lemma">

<a id="radial:axis-lengths"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Scaled affine jets). *Assume <a href="#radial:kappa-assumptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:kappa-assumptions">[radial:kappa-assumptions]</a> and $\lambda\ge1$. Suppose $Y\ge1$, $Y\ge t$, the first two derivatives of $\kappa$ in $\log Y$ are bounded by a fixed constant, and <a id="radial:p-close"></a>$$\label{radial:p-close}
 |p-1|+\left|\frac{dp}{d\log Y}\right|
      +\left|\frac{d^2p}{d(\log Y)^2}\right|\le\delta,
 \qquad 0<\delta\le\tfrac1{10}.$$ At a point use $G_0$-unit affine axes divided by $\sqrt H$. In these axes $G_0=H^{-1}I$ at the point. The transverse and radial Euclidean axis lengths are $$\label{radial:axis-lengths}
 \sqrt{\frac tY},\qquad
 \sqrt{\frac{t}{Y(1+pY)}}.$$ In these fixed coordinates:*

1.  *derivatives of $\Psi$ and $B$ in orders one through four are bounded by $C/H$ and $C\lambda/H$, respectively;*

2.  *metric jets through order two of $V-\lambda G_0$, with $\lambda$ frozen at its value at the point, are bounded by $C\delta\lambda/H$;*

3.  *derivatives of $u$ in orders one through four are bounded by $C$;*

4.  *if a further potential has Euclidean derivatives in orders two through four bounded by $D_e$, its Hessian and first two metric jets here are bounded by $CD_e/(a_\psi H)$.*

*All constants are independent of dimension.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-radial-scaled"><summary>Proof of stmt-radial-scaled</summary>

*Proof.* The eigenvalues of $G_0$ are $a_\psi$ transversely and $a_\psi(1+pY)$ radially, giving <a href="#radial:axis-lengths" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:axis-lengths">[radial:axis-lengths]</a>. In these axes $dt$ and $d^2t$ have operator norms at most $Ct/Y$, and higher derivatives of $t$ vanish. The first bound includes the fact that the only nonzero first derivative is radial; thus its axis length includes the extra factor $(1+pY)^{-1/2}$.

Set a dot to mean $d/d\nu$. We have $$\dot Y=\kappa Y(1+Y)=O(Y^2),\qquad
 \ddot Y=O(Y^3),\qquad \dddot Y=O(Y^4).$$ The last two estimates use, respectively, the first and second indicated derivatives of $\kappa$. Also $\dot p=O(\delta Y)$ and $\ddot p=O(\delta Y^2)$. Direct differentiation gives $$\begin{aligned}
 a'&=aY/t,\\
 a''&=a\bigl(Y^2-Y+\dot Y\bigr)/t^2,\\
 a'''&=a\bigl(Y^3-3Y^2+2Y+3(Y-1)\dot Y+\ddot Y\bigr)/t^3.
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently $|a^{(j)}|\le Ca(Y/t)^j$ for $0\le j\le3$. The same formulas for $a_\psi$ use $pY$ in place of $Y$ and its derivatives. They imply the corresponding estimate for $a_\psi$, and comparison at the point, where $a=\lambda a_\psi$, improves their difference by the factor $\delta$.

The chain rule for a radial potential in at most four spatial slots is a finite sum of products of its $t$ derivatives and copies of $dt,d^2t$. For a term containing its $l$th $t$ derivative, the product of these latter factors is bounded by $C(t/Y)^l$. Since $B^{(l)}=a^{(l-1)}$, every such product is at most $Cat/Y=C\lambda/H$, and the same argument gives $C/H$ for $\Psi$. The improved difference estimate proves the metric-jet assertion. For $u$, its $t$ derivatives through order four are $O((Y/t)^j)$, by the displayed bounds on $Y$ and its derivatives; the same chain rule proves the third assertion. All sums here have a number of terms bounded in terms of the derivative order, not the dimension.

Finally, the largest axis length in <a href="#radial:axis-lengths" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:axis-lengths">[radial:axis-lengths]</a> is $\sqrt{t/Y}\le1$. Applying two, three, or four such axes to the extra potential therefore gives at most $CD_e t/Y=CD_e/(a_\psi H)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Constant-center aligned estimates

<a id="radial:profile-bounds"></a>
In the remainder of this section the fiber center and severity $E$ are constant, the fiber profile is $q=q_0(x,E)$ from Proposition <a href="#quiet:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="quiet:profile">15</a>, and no shear is added. We use the fiber notation $$x=\log\tau,\quad P=\tau q,\quad r=q_x/q,\quad
 D_q=(q_x+q_{xx})/q.$$ The elementary profile estimates of Lemma <a href="#quiet:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="quiet:scalar">14</a> give $q\ge1$, $r>0$, $D_q\ge r^2$, and, for constants allowed to depend on the fixed profile and $E$, $$\label{radial:profile-bounds}
 \begin{split}
 &D_q\ge c_E\tau\quad(0<\tau\le1),\qquad q\le C_E\quad(0<\tau\le1),\\
 &q/D_q\le C q^2,\qquad q\le C_E(1+\log\tau)\quad(\tau\ge1).
 \end{split}$$ These bounds also follow directly from the positive logarithmic summand of $q_0$: for $\tau\ge1$ it supplies a positive lower bound for $q_x+q_{xx}$, whereas the other summand is bounded in $\tau$ at fixed $E$.

<a id="stmt-radial-aligned"></a>
<div id="radial:aligned" class="proposition">

<a id="radial:aligned-alternatives"></a>
**Proposition 20** (Aligned profile). *There is an absolute $\delta_0>0$ with the following property. Fix $\sigma>0$ and take the severity $E$ sufficiently large in terms of $\sigma$ and the fixed profile. Assume the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#radial:scaled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="radial:scaled">19</a>, with $\delta\le\delta_0$, and also $$\tfrac12\le\kappa\le1.1,\qquad H\ge\sigma,
 \qquad G=G_0+G_e,\qquad
 |\partial^{[j]}G_e|\le\delta/H\quad(0\le j\le2)$$ in its scaled affine axes. Here $G=\partial\bar\partial\psi$ and $\partial^{[j]}$ denotes any real or complex metric jet of order $j$, measured as a multilinear operator. Suppose either $$\label{radial:aligned-alternatives}
 \lambda=1,\qquad\text{or}\qquad
 H\ge M_E(1+\log\lambda)^2,$$ where $M_E$ is sufficiently large depending only on the fixed profile and $E$. Then the constant-center fiber potential defines a positive metric with nonpositive real sectional curvature. Its spatial Schur complement satisfies $h\ge cV$.*

<a id="radial:aligned-margin"></a>
*More precisely, in scaled spatial axes and the tilted logarithmic fiber coordinate of Lemma <a href="#fiber:blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fiber:blocks">11</a>, the contraction of its negative curvature on each Hermitian rank-at-most-two matrix with blocks $(T,b,t_f)$ is bounded below by $$\label{radial:aligned-margin}
 c\left(A t_f^2+W(b,\bar b)
               +\frac{\lambda+\tau}{H}|T|^2
               +P|T|_G^2\right),$$ where $c>0$ can be fixed independently of dimension and the stage parameters once the stated thresholds hold. The coefficient $A$ and the form $W$ are those of Lemma <a href="#fiber:blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fiber:blocks">11</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-radial-aligned"><summary>Proof of stmt-radial-aligned</summary>

<a id="radial:h-margin"></a>
*Proof.* Since $q$ has no spatial dependence, $\mathcal N=G$ and $h=V+\tau G$. Lemma <a href="#radial:scaled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="radial:scaled">19</a> shows that, through two metric jets, $h$ differs from the constant multiple $(\lambda+\tau)V/\lambda$ by at most $C\delta(\lambda+\tau)/H$. In particular $$h\asymp\frac{\lambda+\tau}{H}I,\qquad
 \Gamma^h=O(1).$$ Lemma <a href="#radial:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="radial:curvature">18</a> gives a covariant curvature lower bound $c\lambda|T|^2/H$ for $V$ in these scaled axes. Multiplication of a metric by a positive constant multiplies its covariant curvature by that constant. The curvature difference is at most $C\delta(\lambda+\tau)|T|^2/H$: indeed its fourth-derivative term has this bound, and its cubic contractions have the same bound by the inverse estimate $|h^{-1}|\le CH/(\lambda+\tau)$ and the first-jet bounds. Lemma <a href="#fiber:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fiber:perturbation">13</a> makes this calculation explicit. Reducing $\delta_0$ therefore gives $$\label{radial:h-margin}
 C^h(T,T)\ge c\frac{\lambda+\tau}{H}|T|^2
 \quad(\operatorname{rank}T\le2).$$

<a id="radial:W-normalized"></a>
Let $\theta=\lambda/(\lambda+\tau)$. The identity $\nabla^h(V+\tau G)=0$ implies $$\nabla^h G=\theta\bigl(\nabla^hG-\lambda^{-1}\nabla^hV\bigr),
 \qquad |\nabla^hG|\le C\delta\theta/H.$$ The curvature table gives $A=Pq^2D_q$, vanishing spin and $A_i$ terms, and $K=P\nabla^hG$. In $G$-unit normalization it gives $$\label{radial:W-normalized}
 \frac{W}{Pq}=rI+\mathcal Y,
 \qquad \mathcal Y=V_G(V_G+\tau I)^{-1},
 \qquad \mathcal Y\asymp\theta I.$$ Here $V_G$ denotes $V$ expressed in $G$-unit axes; its eigenvalues are $(1+O(\delta))\lambda$. In particular $W>0$. Spending a fixed small portion of $W$ on the $K$ coupling costs at most $$C\delta^2\frac{P\theta^2}{qH(r+\theta)}|T|^2
 \le C\delta^2\frac{\tau\theta}{H}|T|^2,$$ which is an arbitrarily small portion of <a href="#radial:h-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:h-margin">[radial:h-margin]</a> after reducing $\delta_0$.

<a id="radial:trace-remainder"></a>
It remains to pay for $2t_f(W:T)$. Spending $0.98A t_f^2$ costs $(W:T)^2/(0.98A)$. First suppose the second alternative in <a href="#radial:aligned-alternatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:aligned-alternatives">[radial:aligned-alternatives]</a> holds. Split the two terms in <a href="#radial:W-normalized" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:W-normalized">[radial:W-normalized]</a> using $(a+b)^2\le1.1a^2+11b^2$. The $r$ term costs at most $$\frac{1.1}{0.98}P(\operatorname{tr}_G T)^2.$$ The horizontal curvature contains the positive term $$P\bigl((\operatorname{tr}_G T)^2+|T|_G^2\bigr).$$ As $|\operatorname{tr}_G T|\le\sqrt2|T|_G$, their difference retains more than $P|T|_G^2/2$. The remaining trace cost is at most $$\label{radial:trace-remainder}
 C\frac{P\theta^2}{D_qH^2}|T|^2.$$ For $\tau\le1$, <a href="#radial:profile-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:profile-bounds">[radial:profile-bounds]</a> bounds $P/D_q$ by $C_E$. For $\tau\ge1$, it bounds <a href="#radial:trace-remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:trace-remainder">[radial:trace-remainder]</a> by $$\frac{C_E}{H^2}\tau\theta^2(1+\log\tau)^2|T|^2
 \le \frac{C_E(1+\log\lambda)^2}{H^2}
             (\lambda+\tau)|T|^2.$$ For the last inequality, put $z=\tau/\lambda$ and use boundedness of $z(1+z)^{-3}$ and $z(1+z)^{-3}(\log z)^2$ on $(0,\infty)$. Taking $M_E$ sufficiently large absorbs this cost into a small portion of <a href="#radial:h-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:h-margin">[radial:h-margin]</a>.

<a id="radial:lambda-one-slope"></a>
Now suppose $\lambda=1$. The profile estimates give, for sufficiently large $E$, $$\label{radial:lambda-one-slope}
 \|\mathcal Y\|\le r/18\qquad(\tau>2e^{-E}).$$ To see the uniformity of this assertion, $\|\mathcal Y\|\le(1+C\delta)/(1+\tau)$. On $2e^{-E}\le\tau\le1$, the first summand of $q_0$ gives a logarithmic slope bounded below by a large fixed multiple of one. On $1\le\tau\le e^{3b_0E}$, it gives $r\ge0.9b_0(1+\tau)^{-1/16}$ after increasing $E$. Beyond this interval the logarithmic summand gives $r\ge c/q$, which exceeds $40/(1+\tau)$ by its linear-in-$\log\tau$ upper bound and the starting size $\tau\ge e^{3b_0E}$. These statements are unchanged by taking $\delta_0$ smaller.

By <a href="#radial:lambda-one-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:lambda-one-slope">[radial:lambda-one-slope]</a>, the trace expenditure outside the exceptional interval is at most $$\frac{P}{0.98}
       \left(|\operatorname{tr}_GT|+\frac{\sqrt2}{18}|T|_G\right)^2.$$ For $0\le s\le\sqrt2$, the ratio $(s+\sqrt2/18)^2/[0.98(1+s^2)]$ is strictly less than one, by an absolute amount. Thus a fixed portion of $P|T|_G^2$ remains. On $\tau\le2e^{-E}$, the explicit left-end profile gives $q\le C$, $r\le C$, and $D_q\ge c\tau e^E$, where constants may depend on the fixed profile but not $E$. Hence the trace cost is at most $Ce^{-E}|T|^2/H^2$. Since $H\ge\sigma$, this is a small portion of $(1+\tau)|T|^2/H$ for $E$ sufficiently large in terms of $\sigma$.

All cases retain fixed portions of $A$, $W$, <a href="#radial:h-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:h-margin">[radial:h-margin]</a>, and the symmetrized $PG^2$ term, proving <a href="#radial:aligned-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="radial:aligned-margin">[radial:aligned-margin]</a>. Lemma <a href="#fiber:ranktwo" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fiber:ranktwo">12</a> gives nonpositive real sectional curvature off the fiber axis. The metric is positive there and $h\ge cV$. Smoothness of the original affine fiber potential and its positive limiting metric extend both conclusions across the axis. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:centers"></a>
### Changing the fiber center

A moving center is not a holomorphic change of coordinates. We therefore first widen the fibers, then estimate the actual change of potential as a perturbation in fixed holomorphic coordinates. The widening and the metric scales must be chosen in the order specified below.

<a id="center:old-data"></a>
Throughout this section $p=\kappa=1$ and $Y\ge\max\{1,t\}$, so $\lambda,H$ are constant by Lemma <a href="#radial:odes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="radial:odes">17</a>, and $V=\lambda G_0$ as metrics, including their jets. We use the scaled affine spatial coordinates of Lemma <a href="#radial:scaled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="radial:scaled">19</a>. The severity $E$ is constant, and no shear term is present. The old twist has the form $$\psi_{\rm old}=\Psi+\psi_e,
 \qquad G_e=\partial\bar\partial\psi_e,
 \qquad |\partial^{[j]}G_e|\le\delta/H\quad(0\le j\le2),$$ with $\delta$ sufficiently small as in Proposition <a href="#radial:aligned" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="radial:aligned">20</a>. Suppose also that, throughout the interval being configured, $$\label{center:old-data}
 \psi_{\rm old}\le A_{\rm old},\qquad
 |\partial^{[j]}\psi_{\rm old}|\le A_{\rm old}
 \quad(1\le j\le4)$$ in these fixed scaled axes at each point. The number $A_{\rm old}$ is known independently of the target sizes of $\lambda,H$. Additive constants already placed in the twist are included in this upper bound.

<a id="stmt-center-change"></a>
<div id="center:change" class="proposition">

**Proposition 21** (Center change). *Choose the profile constants in the following order: first the fixed exponent $b_0$ of the quiet profile; then a sufficiently large absolute constant $d_0$; and then $K_0$ sufficiently large in terms of $d_0$ and absolute constants specified in the proof. Fix $E$ and old data as in <a href="#center:old-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="center:old-data">[center:old-data]</a>. There is a width parameter $L$, chosen using only these data and the fixed profile, such that the following construction is possible.*

<a id="center:target-order"></a>
*After choosing $L$, choose a sufficiently large $\lambda$, and then a sufficiently large $H$; explicitly one can require $$\label{center:target-order}
 \lambda\ge\max\{e^{3L},\Lambda_f\},
 \qquad H\ge M_f(1+\log\lambda)^2,$$ where $\Lambda_f,M_f$ are finite constants determined by the fixed profile, $E,L$, the old data, and $\delta$, but not by $\lambda,H$. Over three fixed units of the variable $\Psi$, one can change from any prescribed constant center in the unit disk to any other such center, returning to the original profile $q_0(x,E)$ at the end. The final twist differs from the old twist by a nonnegative constant, possibly very large.*

*During the change the potential is smooth, its metric is positive and has nonpositive real sectional curvature, and the metric bounds $cV$ from below on spatial projection, for a fixed $c>0$. There is no restriction on the cost of the final constant offset. All power exponents and smallness tolerances used before choosing the spatial dimension are dimension independent.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The profile deformation and its elementary bounds

<a id="center:q-properties"></a>
Write $F^0_x=1/q_0$ and normalize $F^0(x)-x\to0$ at the left end. We record properties of the concrete quiet profile that will be used here. At fixed $E$, $$\label{center:q-properties}
 \begin{split}
 &q_0\ge1,\quad (q_0)_x>0,\quad
       (q_0)_{xx}/(q_0)_x\ge-1/16,\\
 &|(q_0)_x|+|(q_0)_{xx}|\le C q_0,\qquad
       q_0(x,E)\le C_E+C(1+\max(x,0)),\\
 &\inf_{x\ge-20}(q_0)_x>0.
 \end{split}$$ Here and below a subscript $E$ permits dependence on $E$ and the already fixed profile. The last infimum can be bounded below using just the derivative of $K_0^{-1}\log(1+e^x)$. The inequality for the second derivative follows by differentiating both summands in the quiet profile. In particular $(q_0)_x+(q_0)_{xx}\ge(15/16)(q_0)_x$.

<a id="center:left-slope"></a>
<a id="center:primitive-asymptotic"></a>
Two endpoint properties are also needed. The function $Q_0(v)=q_0(\log v,E)$ is smooth down to $v=0$, with $$\label{center:left-slope}
 Q_0(0)=1,\qquad Q_0'(0)=b_0e^E+K_0^{-1}>0.$$ At the other end the auxiliary function in the quiet profile tends to a positive finite limit with error $O(e^{-x/16})$, so $$q_0(x,E)=c_E+x/K_0+O_E(e^{-x/16})\quad(x\longrightarrow\infty).$$ Integration, using $1/q_0-K_0/x=O_E(x^{-2})$, gives $$\label{center:primitive-asymptotic}
 F^0(x)=K_0\log x+O_E(1),\qquad
 \int_0^L\frac{dx}{q_0(x,E)}=K_0\log L+O_E(1).$$ No uniformity in $E$ is asserted or needed in these endpoint constants; $E$ has been fixed before $L$.

<a id="center:map-bounds"></a>
<a id="center:q1-positive"></a>
Take $L$ large. Choose a smooth function $\chi$ with $$0\le\chi\le1,\quad \operatorname{supp}\chi\subset[-20,3L],\quad
 \chi=1\text{ on }[0,L],\quad \int\chi=2L,\quad \chi'\ge-1/16,$$ and with its derivatives through any fixed needed order bounded absolutely. Such a function is obtained from a fixed rising cutoff, an adjustable plateau, and a fixed descending cutoff of length greater than $16$; adjusting the plateau gives the integral exactly. Define the increasing inverse map $m=m(x)$ by <a id="center:inverse-map"></a>$$\label{center:inverse-map}
 x=m+L-\frac12\int_{-\infty}^m\chi(v)\,dv.$$ Then $$\label{center:map-bounds}
 1\le m'\le2,\quad m\le x,\quad
 m=x-L\ (x\le L-20),\quad m=x\ (x\ge X:=3L).$$ Its derivatives of any fixed order are bounded absolutely. Set $$q_1(x)=q_0(m(x),E)\le q_0(x,E).$$ Since $m''=\chi'(m)(m')^3/2$, putting $a=m'\in[1,2]$ gives $$\begin{aligned}
\label{center:q1-positive}
 (q_1)_x+(q_1)_{xx}
 &\ge(q_0)_x(m)\bigl(a-a^2/16-a^3/32\bigr)\\
 &\ge\tfrac34(q_0)_x(m)a=\tfrac34(q_1)_x>0.\notag
\end{aligned}$$ Also $|(q_1)_x|+|(q_1)_{xx}|\le Cq_1$, with constants independent of $L$. These bounds follow from <a href="#center:q-properties" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="center:q-properties">[center:q-properties]</a> and the bounds on $m',m''$.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Offsets during profile interpolation

On the first unit of $\Psi$, choose a smooth nondecreasing flat-ended cutoff $\beta=\beta(\Psi)$ from $0$ to $1$, with fixed bounds on its derivatives through order four. Define <a id="center:forward-profile"></a>$$\label{center:forward-profile}
 q(x,\beta)=(1-\beta)q_0(x,E)+\beta q_1(x),\qquad
 \mathfrak s=1/q,$$ and let $F^\beta$ have derivative $F^\beta_x=\mathfrak s$ and left normalization $F^\beta-x\to0$. Every such primitive exists, tends to infinity on the right, and differs from $F^0$ by a constant for $x\ge X$. All spatial derivatives in the rest of this subsection are at fixed $\tau=e^x$.

<a id="center:offset-definition"></a>
<a id="center:D-domination"></a>
Use an offset $w^+(\beta)$ defined by $w^+(0)=0$ and $$\label{center:offset-definition}
 (w^+)'(\beta)=\partial_\beta F^\beta(X)
                  +\frac{d_0}{1-\beta+1/L},\qquad
 D(x,\beta)=(w^+)'(\beta)-\partial_\beta F^\beta(x).$$ Here the prime on $w^+$ denotes differentiation in $\beta$. Since $\mathfrak s_\beta\ge0$ and vanishes for $x\ge X$, $$\label{center:D-domination}
 D\ge\frac{d_0}{1-\beta+1/L}.$$ This lower bound can dominate $\sup_x|\mathfrak s_\beta|$ by any required fixed factor if $d_0$ is sufficiently large. Indeed $$0\le\mathfrak s_\beta=(q_0-q_1)/q^2
 \le\min\{(1-\beta)^{-1},\sup_{x\le X}q_0(x,E)\}.$$ By taking $L$ sufficiently large relative to $C_E$, the second entry is at most $CL$ with an absolute $C$. The elementary bound $\min\{z^{-1},CL\}\le(C+1)/(z+1/L)$ proves the assertion, including $\beta=1$ by continuity.

For later restoration use, on another unit of $\Psi$, the reverse mixture $q=(1-\beta)q_1+\beta q_0$ with a fresh increasing cutoff. Now $\mathfrak s_\beta\le0$ and $\partial_\beta F^\beta\le0$. Choose a new offset $w^-(\beta)$ with $w^-(0)=0$ and constant positive derivative greater than a sufficiently large fixed multiple of $1+\sup_{x,\beta}|\mathfrak s_\beta|$. With $D=(w^-)'-\partial_\beta F^\beta$, the same domination holds. No estimate on the size of $w^-(1)$ is required.

Once $L$ is fixed, all the following constants are finite and may be included in a number $C_f$: upper bounds for the offsets and their derivatives through order four; for $D$ and its first three $\beta$ derivatives; for the profile and the finite-order derivatives used below on $x\le X$; for their left-end decay coefficients; and the reciprocal lower constants in the positive estimates on that interval. To check finiteness at the noncompact left endpoint, both $q_0-1$ and $q_1-1$, with their needed derivatives, are $O_f(e^x)$. Thus $\beta$ derivatives of $\mathfrak s$ are $O_f(e^x)$, and their integrals defining derivatives of $F^\beta$ converge. In particular $C_f$ may include $e^X$ and large functions of $E,L$. It is selected before $\lambda,H$.

<a id="stmt-center-interpolation"></a>
<div id="center:interpolation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22** (Curvature during interpolation). *During either profile switch above, keep the center constant and set $\psi=\psi_{\rm old}+w^\pm(\beta(\Psi))$, also including the already frozen offset when making the reverse switch. If $\lambda$ and then $H$ satisfy sufficiently large thresholds of the form <a href="#center:target-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="center:target-order">[center:target-order]</a>, the metric is positive, $h\ge cV$, and its negative curvature has a strictly positive rank-two reserve off the fiber axis. These conclusions extend across that axis by continuity.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-center-interpolation"><summary>Proof of stmt-center-interpolation</summary>

*Proof.* A prime on $\beta$ now means differentiation in $\Psi$. Direct differentiation of $\psi-F^\beta$ gives <a id="center:N-formula"></a>$$\label{center:N-formula}
 \mathcal N=(1+D\beta')G_0+G_e
       +\bigl(D\beta''+D_\beta(\beta')^2\bigr)
                   \partial\Psi\otimes\bar\partial\Psi.$$ Here $D_\beta$ is the derivative at fixed $x$; thus all dependence of the primitive on the spatial cutoff is included. The last term and its first two spatial jets are $O(C_f/H^2)$, because all positive derivatives of $\Psi$ in scaled axes are $O(1/H)$. The derivatives of the scalar $D\beta'$ beyond its value are $O(C_f/H)$. With $d_f=1+D\beta'\ge1$, these facts show $$\mathcal N\asymp d_fG_0>0,
 \qquad h=V+\int_0^\tau\mathcal N(v)\,dv\ge cV.$$ The constants in $\asymp$ can be absolute by making $H$ large enough; the value $d_f$ itself is bounded by $C_f$.

<a id="center:finite-x-h"></a>
First consider $x\le X$. Since $\tau\le e^X$, the first two metric jets of $h-V$ are $O(C_f/H)$. Hence $$\label{center:finite-x-h}
 \Gamma^h=\Gamma^{G_0}+O(C_f/\lambda),\qquad
 C^h(T,T)\ge c\lambda|T|^2/H
 \quad(\operatorname{rank}T\le2).$$ The second estimate follows from Lemma <a href="#radial:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="radial:curvature">18</a>, the scaled jet estimates, and the curvature perturbation identity, taking $\lambda\gg C_f$.

<a id="center:W-spin"></a>
The mixed form in Lemma <a href="#fiber:blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fiber:blocks">11</a> is $$\frac{W}{Pq}=(1+r)\mathcal N
       -\tau\mathcal N h^{-1}\mathcal N
       +q^{-1}\partial\bar\partial\log q.$$ The last term equals $$-\mathfrak s_\beta\beta'G_0+O(C_f/H^2).$$ The spin form, divided by $Pq$, equals $$-\mathfrak s_\beta\beta'\nabla_h^{2,0}\Psi+O(C_f/H^2).$$ In fact $q_\beta/q^2=-\mathfrak s_\beta$; the remaining terms contain two first derivatives of $\Psi$. Lemma <a href="#radial:scaled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="radial:scaled">19</a> and <a href="#center:finite-x-h" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="center:finite-x-h">[center:finite-x-h]</a> give $|\nabla_h^{2,0}\Psi|\le C/H$ with absolute $C$. Also $|\tau\mathcal N h^{-1}\mathcal N|\le C_f/(\lambda H)$. Choose $d_0$ first so that the domination in <a href="#center:D-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="center:D-domination">[center:D-domination]</a> is sufficiently strong in comparison with this absolute $C$, and then choose $\lambda,H$ large compared with $C_f$. It follows that $$\label{center:W-spin}
 W\ge c\frac{Pq}{H}d_f I,
 \qquad |\nabla_h^{2,0}P(b,b)|\le\tfrac14 W(b,\bar b).$$ This argument also applies to the reverse mixture, using absolute values of $\mathfrak s_\beta$.

<a id="center:A-control"></a>
By <a href="#center:q1-positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="center:q1-positive">[center:q1-positive]</a> and convexity of the mixture, $$D_q=(q_x+q_{xx})/q\ge c_f\min(1,\tau)\quad(x\le X).$$ Left-end decay and boundedness on the rest of this interval give $$|\partial\log P|+|\partial P_\tau|
       \le C_f\min(1,\tau)/H.$$ It follows from the curvature table that $$\label{center:A-control}
 A=(1+o(1))Pq^2D_q,\qquad
 |A_i|\le C_f P\min(1,\tau)/H,$$ where the relative error tends uniformly to zero as $\lambda,H$ increase. To verify uniformity at $\tau=0$, the subtracted term in $A$ is at most $C_fP^2\min(1,\tau)^2/(\lambda H)$, while the leading term is $Pq^2D_q$. The ratio tends to zero uniformly under the stated choices.
